Wasserstein Distributionally Robust Nonparametric Regression
Changyu Liu, Yuling Jiao, Junhui Wang, Jian Huang

Abstract
Wasserstein distributionally robust optimization (WDRO) strengthens statistical learning under model uncertainty by minimizing the local worst-case risk within a prescribed ambiguity set. Although WDRO has been extensively studied in parametric settings, its theoretical properties in nonparametric frameworks remain underexplored. This paper investigates WDRO for nonparametric regression. We first establish a structural distinction based on the order k k of the Wasserstein distance, showing that k = 1 k=1 induces Lipschitz-type regularization, whereas k > 1 k>1 corresponds to gradient-norm regularization. To address model misspecification, we analyze the excess local worst-case risk, deriving non-asymptotic error bounds for estimators constructed using norm-constrained feedforward neural networks. This analysis is supported by new covering number and approximation bounds that simultaneously control both the function and its gradient. The proposed estimator achieves a convergence rate of n − 2 β / ( d + 2 β ) n^{-2\beta/(d+2\beta)} up to logarithmic factors, where β \beta depends on the target’s smoothness and network parameters. This rate is shown to be minimax optimal under conditions commonly satisfied in high-dimensional settings. Moreover, these bounds on the excess local worst-case risk imply guarantees on the excess natural risk, ensuring robustness against any distribution within the ambiguity set. We show the framework’s generality across regression and classification problems. Simulation studies and an application to the MNIST dataset further illustrate the estimator’s robustness.

1
Introduction

Consider a nonparametric regression model

Y=f_{0}(X)+\varepsilon,

(1)

where
Y\in\mathbb{R}
is a response,
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a
d
-dimensional vector of covariates,

f_{0}:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is an unknown regression function, and
\varepsilon
is an unobservable error. Assumptions on
\varepsilon
will be specified for each estimation method below.

Our objective is to estimate the unknown regression function
f_{0}
from a finite sample. While extensive research has been conducted on methods for estimating
f_{0}
in standard settings, model uncertainty remains a significant concern. Such uncertainty can arise from various factors, such as incorrect modeling assumptions, data contamination, or distributional inconsistencies between the training and target data. If model uncertainty is not properly addressed, estimators built on incorrect models may exhibit poor generalization performance. To address this problem, we consider a distributionally robust nonparametric estimator, derived by solving the following optimization problem:

\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\Big\{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta):=\sup_{\mathbb{Q}:W_{k}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\ell(Y-f(X))\right]\Big\},

(2)

where
\ell(\cdot)
is a loss function,
W_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the Wasserstein distance of order
k
, and
\mathcal{F}_{n}
represents an estimation function space that may vary with
n
. We refer to
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
as the local worst-case risk, where the inner supremum identifies the worst-case distribution within a
\delta
-Wasserstein ball centered at the joint distribution
\mathbb{P}
of
(X,Y)
. This framework addresses model uncertainty by incorporating distributional perturbations within an ambiguity set. By evaluating and minimizing the risk associated with the worst-case distribution, the resulting estimator enhances robustness and reliability in the presence of potential model uncertainties.

The optimization problem (
2
) falls within the broader framework of Wasserstein distributionally robust optimization (WDRO), a powerful approach that has recently emerged for addressing learning and prediction challenges under model uncertainty. WDRO has been successfully applied across diverse domains, including mechanism design
[
26
]
, power systems
[
41
]
, and machine learning
[
38
]
. Despite its broad adoption, the theoretical understanding of WDRO remains limited. Existing studies primarily focus on parametric settings, where WDRO is linked to classical regularized learning methods
[
9
,
10
,
43
,
11
]
.

In nonparametric settings, while WDRO is known to exhibit certain regularization effects for smooth loss functions
[
6
,
20
]
, its generalization properties have yet to be systematically analyzed. Another critical challenge in WDRO analysis is model misspecification. This occurs when the estimation function space
\mathcal{F}_{n}
does not contain the target function
f_{0}
and
fails to approximate it well. This source of uncertainty can result in poor generalization performance, yet it has received limited attention in the WDRO literature.

A related line of research is adversarial risk, which focuses exclusively on uncertainty in the covariates. This targeted scope makes adversarial risk a special case of WDRO, primarily used in adversarial training to defend against adversarial examples
[
51
,
21
,
36
]
. Its generalization properties have also been extensively studied
[
58
,
25
,
2
,
39
,
53
,
33
]
. However, the analysis developed for adversarial risk does not directly transfer to the WDRO framework. Adversarial risk considers only local perturbations, whereas WDRO accounts for distributional perturbations that induce distinct regularization effects and require different analytical strategies.

In this paper, we investigate the generalization properties of the distributionally robust nonparametric estimators. To evaluate the estimator’s performance under model misspecification, we define the excess local worst-case risk as

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)-\inf_{f\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}\cup\mathcal{F}_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta),

where
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
denotes the Hölder class with smoothness level
\alpha
.
We show that for any distribution
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
satisfying
W_{k}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta
, the following holds.

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f)=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f)-\inf_{f\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)+\delta\|\ell\|_{\operatorname{Lip}},

where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}[\ell(Y-f(X))]
is the natural risk under distribution
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
and
\|\ell\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}
denotes the Lipschitz constant of the loss function.
Thus, the excess local worst-case risk provides an upper bound on the excess natural risk for any distribution within the ambiguity set, illustrating that the estimator achieves reasonable performance even under model uncertainty.

Our main contributions are summarized as follows.

(1)

We analyze the theoretical properties of the local worst-case risk
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
and show a structural distinction in its regularization behavior. Ambiguity sets based on the
W_{1}
distance induce Lipschitz-type regularization, primarily influenced by points where the Lipschitz constant is attained. In contrast, for
k>1
, ambiguity sets based on
W_{k}
correspond to a form of gradient-norm regularization.

(2)

We develop key analytical tools for estimators built from norm-constrained feedforward neural networks. In particular, we establish covering-number and approximation bounds that jointly control both the function and its gradient. These results may be of independent interest for problems involving norm-constrained neural networks.

(3)

We establish non-asymptotic error bounds for the proposed estimator, achieving a convergence rate of
n^{-2\beta/(d+2\beta)}
up to logarithmic factors. This rate applies to both the excess local worst-case risk and the excess natural risk under distributional shifts within the ambiguity set. Under conditions commonly satisfied in high-dimensional settings, the rate coincides with the minimax optimal rate for nonparametric regression. The established error bounds also reveal distinct robustness-related structures, showing that
W_{1}
(
k=1
) induces a term linear in
\delta
, while
W_{k}
(
k>1
) introduces more complex higher-order effects.

(4)

We illustrate the applicability of our theoretical results in both regression and classification problems. The framework accommodates unbounded losses in nonparametric least squares regression, provides explicit robustness guarantees for Huber and quantile regression, and extends naturally to binary classification with label noise.

Our results clarify how Wasserstein order shapes regularization and robustness:
W_{1}
enforces Lipschitz control, while higher-order
W_{k}
induces gradient-norm regularization. We provide general tools for norm-constrained neural networks via bounds that jointly control functions and gradients. The non-asymptotic guarantees attain near-minimax rates and make explicit the
robustness trade-offs (linear in
\delta
for
W_{1}
vs. higher-order effects for
W_{k}
), strengthening performance under shift. Our results’ applicability to regression and classification, including least squares regression, Huber and quantile regression, and binary classification, demonstrates its practical relevance.
We also conduct simulation studies and use an application to the MNIST dataset to illustrate the empirical behavior of WDRO regression and classification.

1.1
Related works

In studying the statistical properties of WDRO, extensive work has focused on characterizing the generalization properties of the WDRO estimator. This includes establishing non-asymptotic upper bounds for

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{n},k}(f;\delta),

where
\mathbb{P}
denotes the underlying data distribution and
\mathbb{P}_{n}
denotes the empirical distribution of a random sample with size
n
from
\mathbb{P},
see for example,
[
28
]
and
[
32
]
.

Another important line of research has focused on finite-sample statistical guarantees, typically formulated as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{n},k}(f;\delta)\quad\text{for any }f\in\mathcal{F}_{n},

(3)

with high probability, ensuring that the empirical WDRO objective function provides an upper bound for expected loss under the true distribution
\mathbb{P}
. While increasing
\delta
raises the likelihood of satisfying (
3
), excessively large values of
\delta
may yield overly conservative estimates. Thus, determining the appropriate scale of
\delta
in relation to the sample size is critical.
[
37
]
established (
3
) with
\delta=O(n^{-1/d})
based on measure concentration results in
[
17
]
, with later works improving this rate to
O(n^{-1/2})
in parametric settings
[
43
]
, and relating
\delta
to the function class complexity in more general settings
[
3
,
29
,
1
,
19
]
. However, these studies do not account for misspecification within the estimation function class
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, which could prevent the right-hand side of (
3
) from becoming small when there is a substantial discrepancy between
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and the target function
f_{0}
. This form of model uncertainty suggests that verifying (
3
) alone may be insufficient to ensure reliable performance.

Our work addresses this issue by considering two different measures to characterize the generalization properties of the WDRO estimator. We first focus on the excess local worst-case risk

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)-\inf_{f\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}\cup\mathcal{F}_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta),

which extends the framework in
[
32
]
by incorporating model misspecification. Furthermore, we establish that for any distribution
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
satisfying
W_{k}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta
, the following holds.

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)+\delta\|\ell\|_{\operatorname{Lip}},

which links the natural risk with the local worst-case risk while accounting for model misspecification on both sides of the inequality. This result illustrates WDRO’s ability to address model uncertainty by producing an estimator that performs reliably for any distribution within the ambiguity set.

The optimization problem in (
2
) has been shown to recover various regularized learning methods when applied to specific loss functions and parametric regression models. Notable examples include the square-root Lasso
[
10
]
, support vector machines
[
43
]
, group-square-root Lasso
[
9
]
, and other parametric forms
[
11
]
. In more general nonparametric settings, recent research indicates that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
exhibits regularization effects similar to those in variation regularization methods
[
20
]
. Additionally,
[
6
]
analyzed the asymptotic first-order behavior of the optimal value of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
with respect to
\delta
and explored its influence on optimizer performance.

Notation.

Let the set of positive integers be denoted by
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,...\}
and let
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}.
If
a
and
b
are two quantities, we use
a\lesssim b
or
b\gtrsim a
to denote the statement that
a\leq Cb
for some constant
C>0
. We denote
a\asymp b
when
a\lesssim b\lesssim a
. Let
\lceil a\rceil
denote the smallest integer larger than or equal to quantity
a
. For a vector
\bm{x}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, we use
\|\bm{x}\|_{p}
to denote the
p
-norm of
\bm{x}
. For a function
f
, we denote its supremum norm by
\|f\|_{\infty}
and its gradient by
\nabla f
. For a measurable function
H
, let
\esssup(H)
denote its essential supremum. For any interger
q\in\mathbb{N}
, let
q^{\star}
be the integer such that
1/q+1/q^{\star}=1
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section
1.1
provides a brief review of related works. Section
2
introduces the local worst-case risk and shows its theoretical properties. Section
3
presents the setup and establishes key properties for norm-constrained FNNs. The main results are presented in Section
4
. Section
5
applies these results to nonparametric regression and classification problems. Section
6
presents the numerical experiments.
Concluding remarks are given in Section
7
. Proofs of the results and additional technical details are provided in the Supplementary Material.

2
Wasserstein distributionally robust nonparametric regression

Let
\mathbb{P}
denote the joint distribution of
Z=(X,Y)
under model (
1
) and let the support of
X
be
\mathcal{X}=[0,1]^{d}.

A classical approach to nonparametric regression seeks to find an estimator
\hat{f}_{n}
within an appropriate nonparametric function space by minimizing the expected loss, also referred to as the natural risk:

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\big[\ell(Y-f(X))\big],

(4)

where
\ell:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
is a loss function. Various nonparametric function spaces, such as reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces, B-splines, and deep neural networks, have been employed to estimate
f_{0}
, with the choice of estimation function class typically depending on the form of the loss function and any assumptions about the smoothness or structure of
f_{0}
. Despite these advancements, model uncertainty remains a persistent challenge in nonparametric regression. To illustrate this issue, we present two simple examples.

Example 1
(Contaminated distribution).

Suppose
Y=f_{0}(X)+\varepsilon
, where
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon|X]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{2}]<\infty
. A common approach to estimate
f_{0}
is through least squares regression, which minimizes the expected squared error:

\argmin_{f}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\big[\{Y-f(X)\}^{2}\big]=\argmin_{f}\big\{\mathbb{E}\big[\{f_{0}(X)-f(X)\}^{2}\big]+\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{2}]\big\}=f_{0}.

Now, consider a scenario in which the observed data are contaminated. In particular, the covariates are observed without error,
\tilde{X}=X
, while the observed response is
\tilde{Y}=Y+g(X)
, where
\mathbb{E}[|g(X)|]\leq\delta
. In this case, the solution to the least squares problem becomes
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Y}|\tilde{X}]=f_{0}(X)+g(X),
so directly applying least squares to contaminated data produces biased estimator, which may significantly degrade generalization performance.

This example illustrates a form of Wasserstein contamination
[
60
,
34
]
, which assumes that the observations come from a corrupted distribution within a Wasserstein ball centered at the true distribution. This framework captures more general types of corruption than the classical Huber’s
\delta
-contamination model
[
22
]
, which allows only an
\delta
-fraction of the data to be arbitrarily corrupted.

Example 2
(Distributional inconsistency).

Suppose we observe
(X_{i},Y_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,n
, independently sampled from the model
Y=f_{0}(X)+\varepsilon
.
After deriving an estimator from these observations, our goal is to predict the response for new covariates. In practice, however, the target data may follow a slightly different model,
Y^{\prime}=f_{0}^{\prime}(X^{\prime})+\varepsilon^{\prime}
, where the distribution of
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
differs from that of the observed data.

In such cases, the estimator derived from the original observations may perform poorly, as it does not account for potential distributional shifts. This scenario is commonly encountered in open-world settings, such as natural scene image analysis, where the distributions of training and target data are unknown and may exhibit subtle inconsistencies.

These examples illustrate that estimators that ignore this uncertainty may perform poorly.
Explicitly incorporating uncertainty into the estimation process is therefore important. The WDRO framework offers a systematic solution by defining an ambiguity set around the observed data and producing estimators that remain robust under uncertainty.

Specifically, we define the local worst-case risk to construct a distributionally robust nonparametric estimator for
f_{0}
:

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)=\sup_{\mathbb{Q}:W_{k}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\ell(Y-f(X))\right],

(5)

where
\delta
is a nonnegative constant that determines the level of uncertainty, and
W_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the Wasserstein distance of order
k
, formally defined below.

Definition 2.1
(Wasserstein distance).

Let the space
\mathcal{Z}
be equipped with a norm
\|\cdot\|
. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\mathcal{Z})
denote the space of Borel probability measures on
\mathcal{Z}
with a finite
k
-th moment. The Wasserstein distance of order
k
between two probability measures
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}(\mathcal{Z})
is defined as

\displaystyle W_{k}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})}\mathbb{E}_{(Z,\widetilde{Z})\sim\pi}\big[\|Z-\widetilde{Z}\|^{k}\big]^{1/k},

where the infimum is taken over the set
\Pi(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
consisting of all probability measures on
\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{Z}
with marginals
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, respectively.

By the definition,
W_{k}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\leq W_{q}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
for any
1\leq k\leq q\leq\infty.
This implies that as
k
increases,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
reflects progressively lower levels of uncertainty. Moreover, when
k=\infty
and the norm
\|\cdot\|
is specifically chosen,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
reduces to adversarial risk, which is commonly used in the training process to defend against adversarial examples
[
51
,
21
,
36
]
.

The level of uncertainty within
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
is determined by
\delta.
When
\delta=0
, no uncertainty is considered, resulting in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;0)=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)
, where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)
represents the natural risk (
4
). However, when
\delta>0
, analyzing
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
becomes challenging due to optimization over an infinite-dimensional space of probability measures. Fortunately, a tractable reformulation of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
is enabled by a strong duality result
[
46
,
59
]
, as shown below. For notational simplicity, let
\bm{z}=(\bm{x},y)
and
\ell(\bm{z};f)=\ell(y-f(\bm{x}))
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
\ell(\bm{z};f)
is a continuous function defined over the normed space
(\mathcal{Z},\|\cdot\|)
. Define
\varphi_{\gamma,k}(\bm{z};f)=\sup_{\bm{z}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}}\{\ell(\bm{z}^{\prime};f)-\gamma\|\bm{z}^{\prime}-\bm{z}\|^{k}\}.
For any
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbb{Q}:W_{k}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\ell(Z;f)\right]=\inf_{\gamma\geq 0}\big\{\gamma\delta^{k}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\varphi_{\gamma,k}(Z;f)\right]\big\},

(6)

and for any
\gamma\geq 0
, there holds

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbb{Q}}\big\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\ell(Z;f)\right]-\gamma W_{k}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})^{k}\big\}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\varphi_{\gamma,k}(Z;f)].

As demonstrated in (
6
),
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
can be reformulated as a univariate minimization problem, enabling tractable convex programs for many common loss functions and estimation function spaces, thus facilitating numerical computation
[
46
,
43
]
. Furthermore, when the regression function is assumed to follow specific parametric structures,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
is directly connected to various classical regularized learning methods. For instance, consider the case of linear regression where
f(\bm{x})=\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\beta}
,
\ell(\bm{z};\bm{\beta})=(y-\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}
, and
\|\bm{z}-\bm{z}^{\prime}\|=\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\|_{q}+\infty 1{\{y\neq y^{\prime}\}}
. Then, minimizing
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},2}(f;\delta)
is equivalent to solving

\min_{\bm{\beta}}\big\{(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\ell(Z;\bm{\beta})])^{1/2}+\delta\|\bm{\beta}\|_{q^{\star}}\big\}^{2},

where
1/q^{\star}+1/q=1

[
10
]
. This shows that the robustness achieved through the local worst-case risk is enabled by norm regularization of the parameter. Similar norm regularization effects of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
on the model parameters can also be observed in other parametric settings
[
11
]
.

However, the exact regularization form of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
does not generally hold, especially in nonparametric settings,
making the analysis of distributionally robust nonparametric estimators more challenging. The following result illustrates a connection between
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},1}(f;\delta)
and regularization on the gradient of the loss function.

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose the composed loss
\ell(\cdot;f)
has a bounded Lipschitz constant
\|\ell_{f}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}<\infty
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},1}(f;\delta)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)\leq\delta\|\ell_{f}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}.

Furthermore, there holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},1}(f;\delta)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)=\delta\|\ell_{f}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}},

if one of the following conditions is satisfied:

(i)

There exists
\bm{z}_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}
such that

{\lim\sup}_{\|\bm{z}-\bm{z}_{0}\|\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\ell(\bm{z};f)-\ell(\bm{z}_{0};f)}{\|\bm{z}-\bm{z}_{0}\|}=\|\ell_{f}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}.

(ii)

There exists
\bm{z}_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}
, a positive constant
\tau
, and a sequence
\{r_{m}\}_{m\geq 1}\rightarrow 0
such that

{\sup}_{\|\bm{z}-\bm{z}_{0}\|\geq\tau}\frac{\ell(\bm{z};f)-\ell(\bm{z}_{0};f)}{\|\bm{z}-\bm{z}_{0}\|}=\|\ell_{f}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}

and
P(\|Z-\bm{z}_{0}\|\leq r_{m})>0
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

This lemma shows that the maximum deviation between
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},1}(f;\delta)
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)
is determined by
\delta\|\ell_{f}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}
. This deviation can be realized in both unbounded (condition (i)) and bounded (condition (ii)) scenarios. The unbounded case is covered in
[
20
, Theorem 2]
. However, when
\mathcal{Z}
is bounded, condition (i) no longer holds. Condition (ii) indicates that the maximum deviation can still be achieved if the Lipschitz constant is attained outside a neighborhood of
\bm{z}_{0}
and the probability of
Z
taking values within any small neighborhood of
\bm{z}_{0}
remains positive.

When there is additional control over the growth rate of
\nabla\ell(\bm{z};f)
, a lower bound for
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},1}(f;\delta)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)
can be further derived. For the remainder of this paper, we focus on the Wasserstein distance induced by the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
norm, and the Lipschitz constant defined with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose there exists a positive number
q>0
and a measurable function
H(\bm{z};f)
such that for any
\tilde{\bm{z}},\bm{z}\in\mathcal{Z},
there holds

\|\nabla\ell(\tilde{\bm{z}};f)-\nabla\ell(\bm{z};f)\|_{1}\leq H(\bm{z};f)\|\tilde{\bm{z}}-\bm{z}\|_{\infty}^{q}.

Let
\mathbb{A}=\{\bm{z}:\|\nabla\ell(\bm{z};f)\|_{1}=\esssup(\|\nabla\ell(Z;f)\|_{1})\}
. Suppose
\mathbb{E}[H(Z;f)1\{Z\in\mathbb{A}\}]<\infty
and
P(Z\in\mathbb{A})>0
, then

\displaystyle\delta\esssup(\|\nabla\ell(Z;f)\|_{1})-\delta^{1+q}\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[H(Z;f)1\{Z\in\mathbb{A}\}\big]}{P(Z\in\mathbb{A})^{1+q}}\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},1}(f;\delta)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)\leq\delta\|\ell_{f}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}.

Lemmas
2.3
and
2.4
show that the performance of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},1}(f;\delta)
is largely determined by the points where the Lipschitz constant is attained. This differs from adversarial risk, which is mainly influenced by the local behavior around each input. The key distinction is that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},1}(f;\delta)
accounts for distributional perturbations that can assign adjustable weights to specific points, whereas adversarial risk focuses on local point perturbations.

For the case where
k\in(1,\infty)
, the performance of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
depends more on the overall behavior of the loss function.

Lemma 2.5
.

Suppose there exists a measurable function
H(\bm{z};f)
such that for any
\tilde{\bm{z}},\bm{z}\in\mathcal{Z},
there holds

\big\|\nabla\ell(\tilde{\bm{z}};f)-\nabla\ell(\bm{z};f)\big\|_{1}\leq H(\bm{z};f)\big\|\tilde{\bm{z}}-\bm{z}\big\|_{\infty}^{\min\{1,k-1\}}.

When
k\in(2,\infty)
and
H(\bm{z};f)\in L^{\frac{k}{k-2}}(\mathbb{P})
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)-\delta\mathbb{E}\big[\|\nabla\ell(Z;f)\|_{1}^{k^{\star}}\big]^{1/k^{\star}}\big|\leq\delta^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[H(Z;f)^{\frac{k}{k-2}}\big]^{1-\frac{2}{k}}.

When
k\in(1,2]
and
H(\bm{z};f)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{P})
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)-\delta\mathbb{E}\big[\|\nabla\ell(Z;f)\|_{1}^{k^{\star}}\big]^{1/k^{\star}}\big|\leq\delta^{k}\esssup\big(H(Z;f)\big).

Lemma
2.5
shows that for small
\delta
and under controlled growth conditions for
\nabla\ell(\bm{z};f)
, the deviation between
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)
approaches
\delta\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\ell(Z;f)\|_{1}^{k^{\star}}]^{1/k^{\star}}
. This result indicates that the extra uncertainty introduced by
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)
can be intuitively interpreted as a form of gradient-norm regularization. Although a similar conclusion appears in Lemma 1 of
[
20
]
, Lemma
2.5
employs a distinct proof approach based on the sensitivity analysis framework of
[
6
]
, which is originally developed to characterize the first-order asymptotic behavior of the WDRO optimal value.

3
Distributionally robust nonparametric estimator

Our target function is defined as

f_{k,\delta}^{\star}=\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta),

(7)

where the local worst-case risk is minimized over the Hölder class with smoothness level
\alpha
.

Definition 3.1
(Hölder class).

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha=r+\beta>0
, where
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\beta\in(0,1]
. The Hölder class
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
is defined as

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}=\left\{f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\max_{\|\bm{s}\|_{1}\leq r}\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\partial^{\bm{s}}f(\bm{x})\right|\leq 1,\max_{\|\bm{s}\|_{1}=r}\sup_{\bm{x}_{1}\neq\bm{x}_{2}}\frac{\left|\partial^{\bm{s}}f(\bm{x}_{1})-\partial^{\bm{s}}f(\bm{x}_{2})\right|}{\|\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{2}\|_{\infty}^{\beta}}\leq 1\right\},\end{split}

where
\bm{s}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
. Let
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{I})=\{f:\mathbb{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},f\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}\}
denote the restriction of
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
to a subset
\mathbb{I}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Suppose
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\dots,(X_{n},Y_{n})
are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) samples from the model (
1
). Let
\mathbb{P}_{n}
denote the empirical distribution induced by the samples. The estimator
\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)}
is defined as the solution to the minimization problem of the empirical counterpart of the local worst-case risk:

\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)}\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{n},k}(f;\delta),

where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{n},k}(f;\delta)=\sup_{\mathbb{Q}:W_{k}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}_{n})\leq\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\ell(Y-f(X))]
. Here,

\mathcal{F}_{n}
represents an estimation function space that may vary with
n
. We require
\mathcal{F}_{n}
to be sufficiently large to provide a good approximation to the Hölder class, while remaining computationally efficient for numerical studies. Motivated by the efficient approximation ability and widespread use of feedforward neural networks (FNNs) in high-dimensional settings, we construct
\mathcal{F}_{n}
using FNNs.

3.1
FNNs with norm constraints

An FNN can be represented as

g=g_{L}\circ g_{L-1}\circ\cdots\circ g_{0},

where
g_{\ell}(\bm{x})=\sigma_{m}(\bm{A}_{\ell}\bm{x}+\bm{b}_{\ell})
for
\ell=0,\dots,L-1
, and
g_{L}(\bm{x})=\bm{A}_{L}\bm{x}
. Here
\bm{A}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\ell+1}\times d_{\ell}}
and
\bm{b}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\ell+1}}
denote weight matrices and bias vectors, respectively, with
d_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
,
d_{0}=d
, and
d_{L+1}=1
.
The activation function is defined as
\sigma_{m}(x)=(\max\{x,0\})^{m}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, corresponding to the rectified power unit (RePU) of order
m
, applied componentwise. The RePU is
(m-1)
-times continuously differentiable.
The FNN is then parameterized by
\theta=(\bm{A}_{0},\dots,\bm{A}_{L},\bm{b}_{0},\dots,\bm{b}_{L-1})
. Its width is defined as
W=\max\{d_{1},\dots,d_{L}\}
, depth as
L
, and size as
\mathcal{S}=\sum_{s=0}^{L-1}d_{s+1}(d_{s}+1)
. The class of FNNs with width at most
W
, depth at most
L
, and size at most
\mathcal{S}
is denoted by
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S})
.

We further consider a norm-constrained subclass. For
K>0
, define
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K)\subseteq\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S})
as the class of FNNs whose parameters satisfy

\kappa(\theta)=m^{L}\|\bm{A}_{L}\|_{\infty}\prod_{s=0}^{L-1}\max\{\|(\bm{A}_{s},\bm{b}_{s})\|_{\infty},1\}^{m^{L-s}}\leq K,

where the matrix infinity norm is defined by
\|\bm{A}\|_{\infty}=\max_{i}\sum_{j}|a_{ij}|
for
\bm{A}=(a_{ij})
.
For any
g_{\theta}\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K)
, the Lipschitz constant is bounded by

\|g_{\theta}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}=\sup_{\bm{x}_{1}\neq\bm{x}_{2}}\frac{|g_{\theta}(\bm{x}_{1})-g_{\theta}(\bm{x}_{2})|}{\|\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{2}\|_{\infty}}\leq\kappa(\theta)\leq K.

Hence, all FNNs in
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K)
admit a uniform Lipschitz bound. Moreover, they also satisfy a uniform Hessian bound,

\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\|\nabla^{2}g_{\theta}(\bm{x})\|_{\infty}\leq Lm^{L}K.

A detailed verification is provided in Lemma A.4 of the Supplementary Material. Notably, these norm constraints are not only theoretically motivated but also practically effective for enhancing model stability and robustness in tasks such as image classification
[
12
,
52
]
.

Next, we establish two key properties of
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K)
, its complexity and approximation ability, which provide the foundation for the main theoretical analysis in Section
4
.

Definition 3.2
(Covering number).

Let
(\mathcal{H},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}})
denote a class of functions
\mathcal{H}
equipped with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}
.
For
u>0
, the
u
-covering number
\mathcal{N}\left(u,\mathcal{H},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}\right)
is defined as the smallest cardinality of a
u
-cover of
\mathcal{H}
, where the set
H_{u}
is a
u
-cover of
\mathcal{H}
if for each
h\in\mathcal{H}
, there exists
h^{\prime}\in H_{u}
such that
\|h^{\prime}-h\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq u
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K)
be defined over
\mathcal{X}
. For any
u>0
, the following bounds hold.
(i) For the supremum norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, we have

\begin{split}\log\mathcal{N}(u,\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim\mathcal{S}\log\left(1+Ku^{-1}\right).\end{split}

(ii) If further
m\geq 2
, then

\log\mathcal{N}(u,\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K),\rho_{\infty})\lesssim\mathcal{S}\log\left(1+LKm^{L}u^{-1}\right),

where
\rho_{\infty}
is defined as
\rho_{\infty}(g,\tilde{g})=\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\{|g(\bm{x})-\tilde{g}(\bm{x})|+\|\nabla g(\bm{x})-\nabla\tilde{g}(\bm{x})\|_{1}\}.

Theorem
3.3
establishes complexity bounds for
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K)
. Classical results such as those based on the pseudo-dimension (see, for example
[
8
]
) typically depend only on the network architecture
(W,L)
and ignore parameter norms, which often leads to loose estimates for modern over-parameterized models. In contrast, Theorem
3.3
provides improved bounds by simultaneously incorporating the norm constraint
K
to capture implicit regularization, utilizing
\mathcal{S}
to reflect the effective model size, and enforcing control over both function values and gradients through the metric
\rho_{\infty}
. These features lead to sharper and more practical bounds for the norm-constrained FNNs.

Theorem 3.4
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
, if
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K)
satisfies
W\!\geq\!N
,
\mathcal{S}\!\geq\!N(d+1)
, and
K\!\gtrsim\!\max\{N^{\frac{d+2m+1-2\alpha}{2d}},\sqrt{\log N}\}
, then
there exists
g\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K)
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}|g(\bm{x})-f(\bm{x})|

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-\frac{\min\{d+2m+1,2\alpha\}}{2d}}\sqrt{\log N},

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\|\nabla(g-f)(\bm{x})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-\frac{\min\{d+2m-1,2(\alpha-1)\}}{2d}}\sqrt{\log N}.

Theorem
3.4
establishes simultaneous approximation error bounds for both function values and gradients when approximating functions in
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
with norm-constrained FNNs. Whereas previous results have primarily focused on characterizing rates in terms of width and depth
[
57
,
42
,
35
]
, Theorem
3.4
shows the effect of the norm constraint
K
on the achievable accuracy. Notably, the requirement that
K
must grow with
N
reflects an intrinsic limitation of norm-constrained FNNs, a finding consistent with the lower-bound analyses of
[
24
]
.

Following
[
4
,
56
]
, the proof strategy first approximates the
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
function via an integral representation, which is in turn approximated by norm-constrained FNNs. The resulting piecewise convergence rate, exhibiting a breakpoint at
\alpha=(d+2m+1)/2
, arises from balancing these two sources of approximation error. This theorem improves upon previous results by explicitly incorporating the simultaneous gradient approximation, which is a key component of our analysis in the
k>1
case. For clarity and completeness, we also formally introduce the integral representation class that serves as the intermediate step in the proof strategy.

Remark 3.5
.

Consider functions in the form as

f(\bm{x})=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}\sigma_{m}\big((\bm{x}^{\top},1)\bm{v}\big)d\mu(\bm{v}),

where
\mu
is a signed Radon measure on
\mathbb{S}^{d}=\{\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1\}
, and define the associated norm

\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{P}_{X})}=\inf\Big\{\|\mu\|:f(\bm{x})=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}\sigma_{m}\big((\bm{x}^{\top},1)\bm{v}\big)\,d\mu(\bm{v})\quad\mathbb{P}_{X}\text{-a.e.}\Big\},

with
\|\mu\|
the total variation norm. The class of functions with finite
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
-norm arises naturally as the space of infinitely wide two-layer neural networks, known as the variation space for RePU activations
[
45
]
. This function space is also closely related to the Barron space, originally introduced in
[
5
]
and extended in
[
14
]
, where the above integral representation is established.

This class exhibits favorable approximation properties and, in certain regimes, can circumvent the curse of dimensionality. In particular,
[
45
]
shows that for any
f
with
\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{P}_{X})}<K
and any
0\leq r\leq m
, there exists
g\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,1,W(d+1),mK(d+1)^{m/2})
such that

\sup_{\|\bm{s}\|_{1}\leq r}\big\|D^{\bm{s}}(f-g)\big\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{d})}\;\lesssim\;KW^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2(m-r)+1}{2d}}.

Hence, the norm-constrained FNNs provide uniform Sobolev-type approximation to Barron functions. This also implies that our results can extend to Barron space when approximation error is quantified in this manner.

4
Main results

This section presents our main non-asymptotic error bounds for both the excess local worst-case risk and the excess natural risk under a shifted distribution. To facilitate the analysis, the following assumption is needed.

Assumption 1
.

Suppose
f_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
and there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}>0
such that the following conditions hold for all
f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
.

(i)
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)-\inf_{f\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)\leq c_{1}+c_{2}\|f-f_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

(ii)
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f_{0})\geq c_{3}\|f-f_{0}\|_{2}^{2}
when
\|f-f_{0}\|_{2}\geq c_{4}
.

Assumption
1
establishes a risk–norm equivalence condition linking the excess natural risk to the
L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
-norm
\|f-f_{0}\|_{2}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[|f(X)-f_{0}(X)|^{2}]
, a standard regularity condition for achieving sharp convergence rates in nonparametric regression
[
7
,
16
]
. The upper bound in (i) controls the approximation error, while
c_{1}
accounts for the bias that arises when
f_{0}
is not the exact natural risk minimizer, as in robust estimation settings (see Section
5.2
). Condition (ii) imposes localized strong convexity, which enables effective control of the stochastic error in empirical risk minimization. Assumption
1
holds for a wide class of loss functions, including the quadratic, Huber, and check losses, as explicitly verified in Section
5
.

Remark 4.1
.

The constants
c_{1}
and
c_{4}
in Assumption
1
are introduced to allow for a more general framework. In many standard nonparametric settings,

f_{0}
is the exact minimizer of the natural risk, i.e.,
f_{0}=\arg\min_{f\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)
.
In this situation, no bias is introduced, and one may set
c_{1}=0
and
c_{4}=0
. This holds, for example, for least squares and quantile regression under appropriate conditions, as verified in Section
5
. Conversely, in other settings,
f_{0}
may not be the exact population-level minimizer. This occurs, for example, in robust regression with the Huber loss. In such cases, a bias term appears (captured by
c_{1}>0
), making the constants
c_{1}
and
c_{4}
non-negligible. Both scenarios are formally verified in Section
5
.

For the estimation function space, let
\mathcal{F}_{n}
denote the truncated class
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K)
at level
M_{n}>0
, where
M_{n}
may grow with
n
. Formally,

\mathcal{F}_{n}=\big\{T_{M_{n}}g:\;g\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K)\big\},

where
T_{M_{n}}
is the truncation operator restricting the output to
[-M_{n},M_{n}]
, defined by
T_{M_{n}}y=y
if
|y|\leq M_{n}
and
T_{M_{n}}y=M_{n}(y/|y|)
otherwise. Allowing
M_{n}
to diverge at an appropriate rate ensures that the approximation properties of
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(W,L,\mathcal{S},K)
are essentially preserved. With this choice of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, the estimator
\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)}
is defined as

\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)}\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{n},k}(f;\delta).

(8)

4.1
Non-asymptotic error bounds for excess local worst-case risk

To evaluate the performance of the estimator
\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)}
, we consider the excess local worst-case risk defined by

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)-\inf_{f\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}\cup\mathcal{F}_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta).

This is nonnegative and measures the gap between the estimator’s population-level risk and the optimal achievable risk. Smaller values indicate that the estimator is closer to the theoretical optimum. We analyze the non-asymptotic behavior of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)};\delta)
for the cases
k=1
and
k>1
separately. The analysis builds upon the robustness regularization properties characterized in Lemmas
2.3
and
2.5
.

Theorem 4.2
(Case
k=1
).

Suppose Assumption
1
holds with
\|\ell\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}<\infty
. Assume
m\geq 1
,
W\geq N,\mathcal{S}\geq N(d+1),

K\!\gtrsim\max\{N^{\frac{d+2m+1-2\alpha}{2d}},\sqrt{\log N}\},
and
M_{n}\asymp\log n
. Let
\beta=\min\{(d+2m+1)/2,\alpha\}
. Then, for any
\delta>0
, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P},1}(\widehat{f}_{1,\delta}^{(n)};\delta)\big]&\lesssim c_{1}+c_{4}^{2}+N^{-\frac{2\beta}{d}}\log N+n^{-1}\mathcal{S}\log(Kn)\log n+\delta K\\
&\qquad\;+\exp\{-\mathcal{S}\log(Kn)\}\log n.\end{split}

If further
\mathcal{S}\asymp W\asymp N\asymp n^{\frac{d}{d+2\beta}}
and
K\asymp\max\big\{n^{\frac{d+2m+1-2\alpha}{2(d+2\beta)}},\sqrt{\log n}\big\}
, then

\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P},1}(\widehat{f}_{1,\delta}^{(n)};\delta)\big]\lesssim c_{1}+c_{4}^{2}+n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}+\delta K.

Corollary 4.3
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
4.2
hold, and assume
c_{1}=c_{4}=0
and
\delta=o(n^{-\frac{d+2m+1+2\beta}{2(d+2\beta)}})
. Then,

\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P},1}(\widehat{f}_{1,\delta}^{(n)};\delta)\big]\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}.

Theorem
4.2
establishes a non-asymptotic error bound on the excess local worst-case risk for the case
k=1
. The bound explicitly decomposes the error into several distinct components: a population-level bias (
c_{1}+c_{4}^{2}
), an approximation error (
N^{-\frac{2\beta}{d}}\log N
), a stochastic error (
n^{-1}\mathcal{S}\log(Kn)\log n
), a term linear in the uncertainty level that reflects the cost of robustness (
\delta K
), and a residual term that decays exponentially.

Corollary
4.3
further shows the convergence rate under optimally chosen tuning parameters. It indicates that, in a bias-free setting (i.e.,
c_{1}=c_{4}=0
) and when
\delta
decreases sufficiently fast, the estimator achieves the convergence rate of
n^{-2\beta/(d+2\beta)}
up to logarithmic factors. This rate is governed by
\beta=\min\{(d+2m+1)/2,\alpha\}
. When
\beta=\alpha
, a condition often satisfied in high-dimensional settings, the rate
n^{-2\alpha/(d+2\alpha)}
matches the minimax optimal rate for nonparametric regression
[
50
]
. In the complementary regime, the rate becomes
n^{-(d+2m+1)/(2d+2m+1)}
, which corresponds to the minimax optimal rate for the Barron space of norm at most
1
when
m=1

[
40
]
. This dual-rate characteristic arises from the proof technique used in Theorem
3.4
, where the Barron space serves as an intermediate function class. The overall convergence is thus dictated by the slower of the two approximation scales. The analysis framework is general and can incorporate sharper approximation bounds for norm-constrained FNNs to further refine the established rate.

Theorem 4.4
(Case
k>1
).

Suppose Assumption 1 holds with
\|\ell^{{}^{\prime}}\|_{\infty},\|\ell^{{}^{\prime}}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}<\infty
. Assume
\alpha>1
,
m\geq 2
,
W\geq N,\mathcal{S}\geq N(d+1),

K\!\gtrsim\max\{N^{\frac{d+2m+1-2\alpha}{2d}},\sqrt{\log N}\},
and
M_{n}\asymp\log n
. Let
\beta=\min\{(d+2m+1)/2,\alpha\}
. Then, for any
\delta>0
, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)};\delta)]&\lesssim c_{1}+c_{4}^{2}+N^{-\frac{2\beta}{d}}\log N+n^{-1}\mathcal{S}\log(Kn)\log n\\
&\qquad+\delta\big\{1+N^{-\frac{\beta-1}{d}}\sqrt{\log N}+\{n^{-1}\mathcal{S}\}^{1/(2k^{\star})}\{Lm^{L}\}^{1/k^{\star}}K\big\}\\
&\qquad+\delta^{\min\{k,2\}}(K^{2}+Lm^{L}K)+\exp\{-\mathcal{S}\log(Kn)\}\log n.\end{split}

If further
\mathcal{S}\asymp W\asymp N\asymp n^{\frac{d}{d+2\beta}}
and
K\asymp\max\big\{n^{\frac{d+2m+1-2\alpha}{2(d+2\beta)}},\sqrt{\log n}\big\}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)};\delta)\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim c_{1}+c_{4}^{2}+n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}+\delta\{1+n^{-\frac{\beta}{k^{\star}(d+2\beta)}}K\}+\delta^{\min\{k,2\}}K^{2}.

Corollary 4.5
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
4.4
hold, and assume
c_{1}=c_{4}=0
and
\delta=o(n^{-\frac{c}{2(d+2\beta)}})
, where
c=\max\{4\beta,2k^{-1}(d+2m+1),2k^{-1}\beta+d+2m+1\}
. Then,

\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)};\delta)\big]\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}.

Theorem
4.4
and Corollary
4.5
consider the case
k>1
and establish a non-asymptotic error bound showing that the estimator can attain the rate
n^{-2\beta/(d+2\beta)}
, up to logarithmic factors, provided that
\delta
decreases sufficiently fast. It is worth noting that the robustness-related terms exhibit a fundamentally different structure compared with the
k=1
case. Instead of the simple
\delta K
term, the bound contains two components,
\delta\{1+n^{-\frac{\beta}{k^{\star}(d+2\beta)}}K\}
and
\delta^{\min\{k,2\}}K^{2}
, corresponding to first-order and higher-order regularization effects. This difference reflects the structural distinctions of the Wasserstein ambiguity sets, with
W_{1}
inducing Lipschitz-type regularization as shown in Lemma
2.3
, and
W_{k}
for
k>1
introducing gradient-norm based higher-order effects as shown in Lemma
2.5
. Taking the limit as
k\to 1^{+}
, the first component converges to
\delta K
, consistent with Theorem
4.2
, whereas the second approaches
\delta K^{2}
, which has no counterpart in the
k=1
analysis as higher-order effects are not considered.

Corollary
4.5
also implies a trade-off between robustness strength and uncertainty tolerance. As
k
increases, the constant
c
in the required decay rate of
\delta
decreases, indicating that larger
k
imposes a weaker constraint on
\delta
. Consequently, the estimator can accommodate a greater level of uncertainty in the
W_{k}
sense while preserving the optimal convergence rate. This behavior arises from the geometry of Wasserstein balls, where for
q>k
the set
\{\mathbb{Q}:W_{k}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\leq\delta\}
contains
\{\mathbb{Q}:W_{q}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\leq\delta\}
. Therefore, smaller values of
k
provide stronger robustness guarantees, whereas larger
k
allow for higher uncertainty levels, showing the need for careful design of robust models.

4.2
Robustness in the presence of model uncertainty

An important property of the distributionally robust nonparametric estimator is its ability to perform reliably across a range of possible distributions, ensuring robust performance even in the presence of model uncertainty. Specifically, we define the excess natural risk as

{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f)=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f)-\inf_{f\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f),

(9)

where the distribution
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
is explicitly included in the notation to emphasize the specific distribution under which the natural risk is defined.

Theorem 4.6
.

Assume
\|\ell\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}<\infty
. For any
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
satisfying
W_{k}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta
, it holds that

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)+\delta\|\ell\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}.

Moreover, under the conditions of Theorem
4.2
for
k=1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(\widehat{f}_{1,\delta}^{(n)})\big]\lesssim c_{1}+c_{4}^{2}+n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}+\delta K.

Alternatively, under the conditions of Theorem
4.4
for
k>1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)})\big]\lesssim c_{1}+c_{4}^{2}+n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}+\delta\{1+n^{-\frac{\beta}{k^{\star}(d+2\beta)}}K\}+\delta^{\min\{k,2\}}K^{2}.

Theorem
4.6
shows that a smaller excess local worst-case risk leads to a correspondingly smaller excess natural risk under any distribution within the ambiguity set. In contrast, existing works
[
37
,
17
,
3
,
43
,
29
,
1
,
19
]
primarily focus on establishing bounds of the form:

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{n},k}(f;\delta).

These works typically aim to identify a suitable
\delta
such that the empirical WDRO objective upper bounds the expected loss under the data-generating distribution. Theorem
4.6
, however, offers a distinct and more comprehensive perspective. It explicitly shows the impact of
\delta
in controlling the excess natural risk under distributional perturbations, while also integrating the effect of model misspecification. This leads to a more detailed and precise evaluation of the estimator’s performance under both distributional and model uncertainty.

Corollary 4.7
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
4.6
hold and
c_{1}=c_{4}=0
. Let
\delta=o(n^{-\frac{d+2m+1+2\beta}{2(d+2\beta)}})
for
k=1
and
\delta=o(n^{-\frac{c}{2(d+2\beta)}})
for
k>1
, where
c=\max\{4\beta,2k^{-1}(d+2m+1),2k^{-1}\beta+d+2m+1\}
. For any
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
satisfying
W_{k}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)})\big]\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}.

Continuation of Examples
1
and
2
.

In the two examples, we illustrate simple cases of contaminated distributions and distributional inconsistency. In these scenarios, the problem arises from the inconsistency between the observation and target data distributions. Specifically, when the target data distribution
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
is located within a
\delta
-Wasserstein ball, Theorem
4.6
shows that the excess natural risk of any estimator
f
under this target distribution is bounded by
{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(f;\delta)+\delta\|\ell\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}.
This result guarantees that the estimator performs well under the target distribution, provided that
f
maintains a small local worst-case risk.

5
Applications

To illustrate the results in Section
4
, we consider four examples: nonparametric least squares regression, nonparametric robust regression, nonparametric quantile regression, and classification. In each example, Assumption
1
is explicitly verified, and the analysis is adapted to the corresponding setting.

5.1
Nonparametric least squares regression

Suppose the error
\varepsilon
in regression model (
1
) satisfies
\mathbb{E}[varepsilon|X]=0

and
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon^{2})<\infty.

Consider the quadratic loss
\ell(u)=u^{2}
, whose natural risk minimizer is the conditional mean, i.e.,
f_{0}=\argmin_{f\text{ measurable}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\{Y-f(X)\}^{2}].
It can be verified that Assumption
1
holds for the quadratic loss with
c_{1}=c_{4}=0
and
c_{2}=c_{3}=1
. The corresponding distributionally robust nonparametric estimator is defined by

\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)}\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\sup_{\mathbb{Q}:W_{k}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}_{n})\leq\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\{Y-f(X)\}^{2}\right].

Although the exact minimizer of the local worst-case risk is generally intractable due to the supremum over an infinite set, some intuition can be gained from the following analysis:

\displaystyle\inf_{f\operatorname{measurable}}\sup_{\mathbb{Q}:W_{k}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\{Y-f(X)\}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\mathbb{Q}:W_{k}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta}\inf_{f\operatorname{measurable}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\{Y-f(X)\}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\mathbb{Q}:W_{k}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\{Y-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[Y|X]\}^{2}\right],

where
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[Y|X]
denotes the conditional expectation under
\mathbb{Q}.
This suggests that the distributional perturbation tends to find a distribution near
\mathbb{P}
that maximizes the variance of the residual
Y-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[Y|X]
. Establishing equality in the above inequalities requires additional conditions, presenting an interesting direction for future research.

Assumption 2
.

There exists a constant
\sigma_{Y}>0
such that
\mathbb{E}[\exp\{\sigma_{Y}|Y|\}]<\infty.

Assumption
2
is a mild condition on
Y
, allowing unbounded cases with sub-exponential tails and relaxing the requirement of strict boundedness. This assumption is important for deriving non-asymptotic error bounds for the excess local worst-case risk under the quadratic loss. The main challenge is controlling the unbounded derivatives of the loss. We address this by employing a truncation technique that restricts the analysis to a bounded region where the derivatives are controlled, while tail behavior is accounted for through Assumption
2
and the Wasserstein constraint. Moreover, standard least squares regression requires
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{2}]<\infty
, and to ensure this condition holds under perturbations within the ambiguity set, we focus on
k\geq 2
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon|X)=0
,
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon^{2})<\infty,

and Assumption
2
holds. Assume
\alpha\!>\!1
,
m\!\geq\!2
,
W\!\geq\!N,\mathcal{S}\!\geq\!N(d+1),

K\!\gtrsim\!\max\{N^{\frac{d+2m+1-2\alpha}{2d}},\sqrt{\log N}\},
and
M_{n}\asymp\log n
. Let
\beta=\min\{(d+2m+1)/2,\alpha\}
. Then, for any
\delta>0
and
k\geq 2
, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)};\delta)\big]&\lesssim N^{-\frac{2\beta}{d}}\log N+n^{-1}\mathcal{S}\log(Kn)\{\log n\}^{3}+n^{-1}\log n\\
&\qquad+\delta\big\{1+N^{-\frac{\beta-1}{d}}\sqrt{\log N}+\{n^{-1}\mathcal{S}\}^{1/(2k^{\star})}\{Lm^{L}\}^{1/k^{\star}}K\big\}\log n\\
&\qquad+\delta^{2}\big\{1+K^{2}+Lm^{L}K\big\}\log n.\end{split}

If further
\mathcal{S}\asymp W\asymp N\asymp n^{\frac{d}{d+2\beta}}
and
K\asymp\max\big\{n^{\frac{d+2m+1-2\alpha}{2(d+2\beta)}},\sqrt{\log n}\big\}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)};\delta)\big]\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{4}+\delta\big\{1+n^{-\frac{\beta}{k^{\star}(d+2\beta)}}K\big\}\log n+\delta^{2}K^{2}\log n.

In particular, if
\delta=o\big(n^{-\frac{c}{2(d+2\beta)}}\big)
, with
c=\max\{4\beta,2k^{-1}\beta+d+2m+1\}
, then

\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal E}_{\mathbb{P},k}(\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)};\delta)\big]\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{4}.

Theorem
5.1
shows that the theoretical results established in Theorem
4.4
extend to least squares regression, despite the quadratic loss not satisfying the bounded derivative condition.
For sufficiently small
\delta
, the proposed estimator achieves a convergence rate of
n^{-2\beta/(d+2\beta)}
, up to logarithmic factors. Further, Theorem
5.1
enables us to derive bounds on the
L^{2}
-error under distributional shifts. Specifically, for any distribution
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
and
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
, let the corresponding target function be
f_{0}^{\prime}(\bm{x})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}[Y^{\prime}|X^{\prime}=\bm{x}],
and define the
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{X})
-norm by
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{X})}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}[|f(X^{\prime})|^{2}]^{1/2}.

Theorem 5.2
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem 5.1 hold and
\delta=o\big(n^{-\frac{c}{2(d+2\beta)}}\big)
, with
c=\max\{4\beta,2k^{-1}\beta+d+2m+1\}
. For any
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
satisfying
W_{k}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta
, and assuming
f_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)}-f_{0}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{X})}^{2}\big]\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{4}.

Theorem
5.2
indicates that for any
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
within the
W_{k}
ambiguity set, the estimator achieves a convergence rate of
n^{-2\beta/(d+2\beta)}
, up to logarithmic factors, in the
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{X})
-norm with respect to its corresponding target function
f_{0}^{\prime}
. As discussed in the general analysis in Section
4
, this rate coincides with the minimax optimal rate for nonparametric regression in
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
when
\alpha
is less than
(d+2m+1)/2
, a condition often satisfied in high-dimensional settings.

Our analysis extends existing work to a distributionally robust framework. Previous studies have shown that least squares regression with deep neural networks can achieve the minimax optimal convergence rate
[
42
,
27
,
23
]
, and norm-constrained FNNs in least-squares settings have been analyzed in
[
24
,
55
]
. In contrast, the present work extends these studies by explicitly incorporating model uncertainty and provides theoretical guarantees under distributional variation.

5.2
Nonparametric robust regression

Suppose the error in the regression model (
1
) satisfies
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon|X]=0.

To reduce the sensitivity of the quadratic loss to outliers in response, a common approach is to consider the Huber loss
\ell_{\tau}(\cdot)
, defined by

\displaystyle\ell_{\tau}(u)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}u^{2},&\qquad|u|\leq\tau,\\
\tau|u|-\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2},&\qquad|u|>\tau,\end{cases}

where
\tau>0
is a tuning parameter. This loss is continuously differentiable, with derivative
\ell_{\tau}^{\prime}(u)=\min\{\max(-\tau,u),\tau\}
. The derivative is 1-Lipschitz and uniformly bounded by
\tau
. To further enhance robustness against model uncertainty, we consider

\widehat{f}_{\delta,\tau}^{(n)}\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\sup_{\mathbb{Q}:W_{k}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}_{n})\leq\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\ell_{\tau}(Y-f(X))].

The statistical properties of the natural risk
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\ell_{\tau}(Y-f(X))]
are characterized by the conditional distribution of
\varepsilon
given
X
, as described below.

Assumption 3
.

(i) There exists
r\geq 1
such that
\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon|^{r}|X=\bm{x}]\leq v_{r}<\infty
for all
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X};

(ii) The conditional distribution of
\varepsilon
given
X
is symmetric around zero.

When only the
r
-th moment condition in Assumption
3
(1) holds,
[
15
]
shows that for any
f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
, if
\tau\geq 2\max\{2M_{n},(2v_{r})^{1/r}\}
, then

-\tau^{1-r}v_{r}\|f-f_{0}\|_{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}\|f-f_{0}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f_{0})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\tau^{2-2r}v_{r}^{2}+\|f-f_{0}\|_{2}^{2},

(10)

where
\|\cdot\|_{2}=\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})}
. In this case, the minimizer
f_{\tau}=\argmin_{f}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)
may differ from
f_{0}
, with the bias bounded by
\|f_{\tau}-f_{0}\|_{2}\leq 4\tau^{1-r}v_{r}
. Combining this with (
10
) further yields

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f_{\tau})\leq 5\tau^{2-2r}v_{r}^{2}+\|f-f_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

This result implies that Assumption
3
(i) ensures Assumption
1
with
c_{1}=5\tau^{2-2r}v_{r}^{2}
,
c_{2}=1
,
c_{3}=1/8
, and
c_{4}=8\tau^{1-r}v_{r}
. Moreover, if the conditional distribution of
\varepsilon
is symmetric, that is, Assumption
3
(ii) also holds, the inequalities in (
10
) simplify to

\tfrac{1}{4}\|f-f_{0}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f_{0})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\|f-f_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

In this symmetric setting, the bias term vanishes, implying that
f_{0}
is the exact population Huber minimizer
f_{\tau}
, and no bias is introduced by the Huberization process. Therefore, when Assumption
3
holds, Assumption
1
is satisfied with
c_{1}=c_{4}=0
,
c_{2}=1/2
, and
c_{3}=1/4
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
4.6
and Assumption
3
hold, and let
\tau\geq 2\max\{2M_{n},(2v_{r})^{1/r}\}
. If
k=1
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\widehat{f}_{\delta,\tau}^{(n)}-f_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})}^{2}\big]\lesssim\tau^{2}n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}+\delta\tau K,

where
K\asymp\max\big\{n^{\frac{d+2m+1-2\alpha}{2(d+2\beta)}},\sqrt{\log n}\big\}
.
Further, if
k>1
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\widehat{f}_{\delta,\tau}^{(n)}-f_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})}^{2}\big]\lesssim\tau^{2}n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}+\delta\tau\{1+n^{-\frac{\beta}{k^{\star}(d+2\beta)}}K\}+\delta^{\min\{k,2\}}\tau K^{2}.

Proposition
5.3
establishes the
L^{2}
convergence rate of the proposed estimator, quantifying the influence of
\tau
. The bounds indicate that both the statistical error and the robustness-related terms grow with
\tau
. While a larger
\tau
is required to accommodate non-outlier data with large magnitudes, it simultaneously reduces the estimator’s robustness by treating larger errors as inliers. Ths causes the theoretical error bound to increase with
\tau
.

A key challenge within this distributionally robust framework is that the noise symmetry guaranteed by Assumption
3
(2) is generally not preserved under distributional perturbations. Consider a target distribution
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
satisfying
W_{k}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta
, and denote the corresponding regression model by
Y^{\prime}=f_{0}^{\prime}(X^{\prime})+\varepsilon^{\prime}
. Even if the nominal noise
\varepsilon
is symmetric, the perturbed noise
\varepsilon^{\prime}
is generally not. For example, a simple perturbation of the form
Y^{\prime}=Y+\lambda\varepsilon_{1}
, where
\varepsilon_{1}
is asymmetric and
\lambda>0
is small, will result in an asymmetric noise
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon+\lambda\varepsilon_{1}
. Consequently,
the Huberization bias, which is absent in the nominal model, reappears under the perturbed distribution. Denote the minimizer of the natural risk under
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
by

f_{0,\tau}^{\prime}=\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}[\ell_{\tau}(Y^{\prime}-f(X^{\prime}))].

When
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
also satisfies the
r
-th moment condition in Assumption
3
(1), the lower bound in (
10
) remains valid. Combined with Young’s inequality, this yields the
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{X})
-norm relation.

\|f-f_{0,\tau}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{\prime})}^{2}\leq 16\left\{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f_{0}^{\prime})+4\tau^{2-2r}v_{r}^{2}\right\},

which serves as the key step in translating the excess natural risk bounds from Theorem
4.6
into an
L^{2}
convergence rate relative to
f_{0,\tau}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
4.6
and Assumption
3
hold, and let
\tau\geq 2\max\{2M_{n},(2v_{r})^{1/r}\}
. For any
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
satisfying
W_{k}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta
, assume that
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
also satisfies
f_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
and Assumption
3
(1). If
k=1
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\widehat{f}_{\delta,\tau}^{(n)}-f_{0,\tau}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{\prime})}^{2}\big]\lesssim\tau^{2-2r}v_{r}^{2}+\tau^{2}n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}+\delta\tau K,

where
K\asymp\max\big\{n^{\frac{d+2m+1-2\alpha}{2(d+2\beta)}},\sqrt{\log n}\big\}
.
Further, if
k>1
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\widehat{f}_{\delta,\tau}^{(n)}-f_{0,\tau}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{\prime})}^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\tau^{2-2r}v_{r}^{2}+\tau^{2}n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\;+\delta\tau\{1+n^{-\frac{\beta}{k^{\star}(d+2\beta)}}K\}+\delta^{\min\{k,2\}}\tau K^{2}.

Proposition
5.4
establishes the
L^{2}
convergence rate relative to
f_{0,\tau}^{\prime}
and explicitly captures the Huberization bias
\tau^{2-2r}v_{r}^{2}
, which arises under asymmetric noise. This bias remains constant for heavy-tailed noise when
r=1
and decays with
\tau
for
r>1
. Since both the statistical and robustness-related error terms increase with
\tau
, its selection requires careful balancing to optimize overall performance.

Corollary 5.5
.

Suppose the conditions of Proposition
5.4
hold. Let
\delta=o(n^{-\frac{d+2m+1+2\beta}{2(d+2\beta)}})
for
k=1
and
\delta=o(n^{-\frac{c}{2(d+2\beta)}})
for
k>1
, where
c=\max\{4\beta,2k^{-1}(d+2m+1),2k^{-1}\beta+d+2m+1\}
. For any
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
satisfying
W_{k}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta
, assume that
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
also satisfies
f_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
and Assumption
3
(1). Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\widehat{f}_{\delta,\tau}^{(n)}-f_{0,\tau}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{\prime})}^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\tau^{2-2r}v_{r}^{2}+\tau^{2}n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}.

5.3
Nonparametric quantile regression

For a given quantile level
\varrho\in(0,1)
, consider the quantile regression model

Y=f_{\varrho}(X)+\varepsilon,

(11)

where
f_{\varrho}
is the true
\varrho
-th conditional quantile of
Y
given
X
, and
\varepsilon
satisfies
P(\varepsilon\leq 0\mid X)=\varrho.
Quantile regression provides a detailed characterization of how
X
affects the conditional distribution of
Y
, which is particularly useful in the presence of skewness or heteroscedasticity. Estimation of
f_{\varrho}
is based on minimizing the natural risk
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)=\mathbb{E}[\ell_{\varrho}(Y-f(X))]
, where
\ell_{\varrho}
is the check loss function:

\displaystyle\ell_{\varrho}(u)=u\big\{\varrho-I(u\leq 0)\big\}.

The loss
\ell_{\varrho}
is convex and Lipschitz continuous with
\|\ell_{\varrho}\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}=\max\{1-\varrho,\varrho\}
, and
f_{\varrho}
is the unique minimizer of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)
under standard regularity conditions. The corresponding distributionally robust nonparametric estimator is defined as

\widehat{f}_{\delta,\varrho}^{(n)}\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\sup_{\mathbb{Q}:W_{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}_{n})\leq\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\ell_{\varrho}(Y-f(X))].

A key component of the analysis is the following assumption on
P_{Y|X=\bm{x}}(\cdot)
, which denotes the conditional cumulative distribution function of
Y
given
X=\bm{x}
.

Assumption 4
.

There exist constants
L,\nu_{1},\nu_{2}>0
such that for all
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle\text{(1)}\quad\big|P_{Y|X=\bm{x}}(f_{\varrho}(\bm{x})+u)-P_{Y|X=\bm{x}}(f_{\varrho}(\bm{x}))\big|

\displaystyle\leq\nu_{1}|u|,\quad\text{for all }u\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\text{(2)}\quad\big|P_{Y|X=\bm{x}}(f_{\varrho}(\bm{x})+u)-P_{Y|X=\bm{x}}(f_{\varrho}(\bm{x}))\big|

\displaystyle\geq\nu_{2}|u|,\quad\text{for all }|u|\leq L.

Assumption
4
characterizes the properties of the conditional distribution around the true quantile. Condition (1) imposes a global Lipschitz constraint on the conditional cumulative distribution function, guaranteeing that condition (i) of Assumption
1
is satisfied with
c_{1}=0
and
c_{2}=\nu_{1}/2
. Condition (2), often referred to as the self-calibration condition
[
44
]
, requires the conditional density of
\varepsilon
near zero to be bounded away from zero, ensuring localized strong convexity of the natural risk. In particular, it implies that condition (ii) of Assumption
1
holds with
c_{4}=0
and
c_{3}=\min\{\nu_{2}/2,\nu_{2}L/(8M_{n})\}
, which simplifies to
c_{3}=\nu_{2}/2
when
\|f-f_{\varrho}\|_{\infty}\leq L
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
4.6
and Assumption
4
hold. For any
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
satisfying
W_{1}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta
, assume that
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
also satisfies Assumption
4
(2) and the corresponding quantile function
f_{\varrho}^{\prime}

satisfies
f_{\varrho}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\widehat{f}_{\delta,\varrho}^{(n)}-f_{\varrho}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{\prime})}^{2}\big]\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{3}+\delta K\log n,

where
K\asymp\max\big\{n^{\frac{d+2m+1-2\alpha}{2(d+2\beta)}},\sqrt{\log n}\big\}
. If further
\delta=o(n^{-\frac{d+2m+1+2\beta}{2(d+2\beta)}})
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\widehat{f}_{\delta,\varrho}^{(n)}-f_{\varrho}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{\prime})}^{2}\big]\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{3}.

5.4
Classification

In a binary classification task, we seek to predict a label
Y\in\{-1,1\}
from covariates
X
. This task is typically evaluated using the classification loss
\ell_{\operatorname{class}}(u,y)=1\big\{\operatorname{sign}(u)y\leq 0\big\},
where
u\in\mathbb{R}
is a score and
\operatorname{sign}(u)=1
if
u\geq 0
and
-1
otherwise. Given a score function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, the associated classifier is
\operatorname{sign}(f)
, and its natural classification risk is defined by

\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{class},\mathbb{P}}(f)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\big[1\big\{\operatorname{sign}f(X)\neq Y\big\}\big].

The minimum natural classification risk, known as the Bayes risk, is

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{class},\mathbb{P}}^{\star}=\inf_{f\text{ measurable}}\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{class},\mathbb{P}}(f)=\mathbb{E}\big[\min\{\eta(X),1-\eta(X)\}\big],

where
\eta(\bm{x})=P(Y=1|X=\bm{x})
is the conditional class probability. This infimum is reached by Bayes regression function
f^{\star}(\bm{x})=2\eta(\bm{x})-1

[
49
]
.

Since the classification loss is non-smooth and non-convex, its direct minimization is computationally intractable. This motivates the use of margin-based surrogate losses, which take the form
\ell(u,y)=\phi(uy)
for some convex margin loss
\phi:\mathbb{R}\mapsto[0,\infty)
. A primary requirement for selecting
\phi
is to ensure classification consistency
[
49
]
, meaning that minimizing the surrogate risk yields a classifier converging to the Bayes optimal classifier
\operatorname{sign}(f^{\star})
. Although margin-based methods have been extensively studied, standard estimators remain sensitive to label noise, which may arise from measurement error or other sources of uncertainty. This type of error is distinct from the covariate perturbations typically considered in adversarial risk. To address uncertainty in both
X
and
Y
, we consider the distributionally robust nonparametric estimator

\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)}\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\sup_{\mathbb{Q}:W_{k}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}_{n})\leq\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\phi(f(X)Y)].

(12)

Although this multiplicative loss form
\phi(f(X)Y)
differs from the additive structure in our main analysis, the framework can be extended. For simplicity, we focus on the quadratic surrogate
\phi(u)=(1-u)^{2}
, which connects the classification and least-squares frameworks. The corresponding risk satisfies
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}}(f)=\mathbb{E}[(1-f(X)Y)^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[(Y-f(X))^{2}],
since
Y^{2}=1
. Thus, its population minimizer
f_{0}(\bm{x})=\mathbb{E}[Y|X=\bm{x}]
is exactly the Bayes regression function
f^{\star}(\bm{x})
. This equivalence allows us to apply the
L^{2}
error bounds established in Theorem
5.2
. For any
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
satisfying
W_{k}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta
, let
f_{0}^{\prime}(\bm{x})=\mathbb{E}[Y^{\prime}|X^{\prime}=\bm{x}]
denote its corresponding Bayes regression function. Assuming
f_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
, the results of Theorem
5.2
yield the following
L^{2}
convergence rate.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)}-f_{0}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{X})}^{2}\big]\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{4}.

While this
L^{2}
bound is a direct consequence of the previous analysis, the primary goal in classification is to bound the natural classification risk. The excess classification risk is related to the
L^{2}
error via the following inequality
[
49
]
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{class},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(f)-\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{class},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}^{\star}\leq\|f-f_{0}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{\prime})}.

Combining these results yields the following result for the excess classification risk.

Proposition 5.7
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
5.2
hold. For any
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
satisfying
W_{k}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta
, and assuming its Bayes regression function satisfies
f_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{class},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)})-\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{class},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}^{\star}\big]\lesssim n^{-\frac{\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}.

This proposition is particularly relevant for learning under label noise. Let
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{true}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{obs}}
denote the true and observed distributions, respectively, where
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{obs}}
coincides with
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{true}}
except that labels are independently flipped with probability
\xi\in(0,1)
. In this case, the Wasserstein distance between the two distributions is bounded by
W_{k}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{true}},\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{obs}})\leq 2\xi^{1/k}
. If the estimator
\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)}
is trained on samples from
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{obs}}
and we choose an uncertainty level
\delta\geq 2\xi^{1/k}
, then
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{true}}
is ensured to be in the ambiguity set. Proposition
5.7
is thus directly applicable by setting
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}=\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{true}}
, yielding a bound on the excess classification risk under the true data-generating distribution.

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{true}}
be the true distribution and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{obs}}
be the observed version where labels are misclassified with probability
\xi\in(0,1)
. Let the estimator
\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)}
in (
12
) be constructed from samples from
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{obs}}
with
\delta\geq 2\xi^{1/k}
. Suppose the conditions of Proposition
5.7
hold. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{class},\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{true}}}(\widehat{f}_{k,\delta}^{(n)})-\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{class},\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{true}}}^{\star}\big]\lesssim n^{-\frac{\beta}{d+2\beta}}\{\log n\}^{2}.

This result indicates that, despite being constructed from potentially misclassified data, the distributionally robust nonparametric estimator retains consistency under the true data-generating distribution, provided the uncertainty level
\delta
is chosen to be larger than the noise level. This result is further illustrated in the numerical study in Section
6
.

6
Numerical results

In this section, we conduct numerical experiments to evaluate the finite-sample performance of the WDRO method. Its performance is compared to that of empirical risk minimization (ERM), which optimizes the natural risk, across both regression and classification tasks. To illustrate its practical utility, we also include an application to the MNIST dataset. Additional algorithmic details are provided in the Supplementary Material.

6.1
Simulation

For regression, we considered three loss functions: quadratic loss, Huber loss (with
\tau=1
), and check loss (with
\rho=0.5
). For classification, the cross-entropy loss was used. Model uncertainty was introduced through a shift probability, which controlled the likelihood of replacing a standard training sample with one drawn from a shifted distribution. When the shift probability was set to zero, the training data remained unchanged.

Specifically, the covariate vector
X=(x_{1},\dots,x_{10})^{\top}
consisted of five continuous variables sampled independently from
U(0,1)
and five binary variables sampled independently from
\text{Bernoulli}(0.5)
. The response satisfied
Y=f_{0}(X)+\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon\sim N(0,0.5)
and

f_{0}(X)=3x_{1}^{2}-2x_{2}+4x_{3}x_{7}+2\sin(2\pi x_{4})+\cos(3\pi x_{9})+\exp(x_{5}x_{8})/10.

For classification, the response
Y
was then converted to binary labels: samples with
Y>0
were labeled as class 1, and those with
Y\leq 0
as class
-1
.

To simulate model uncertainty, training samples were replaced according to a specified shift probability. In regression, both
X
and
Y
were perturbed. The first five continuous variables of
X
were modified by adding the vector
(0.3,-0.2,0.25,-0.15,0.2)^{\top}
, followed by clipping to
[0,1]
. Binary variables
x_{6}
and
x_{8}
were flipped with probability
0.5
. The response was then generated from a shifted function

f_{\text{shifted}}(X)=x_{1}^{2}+2x_{3}x_{7}+2\sin(2\pi x_{4})+\cos(3\pi x_{9})+\exp(x_{5}x_{8})/10,

with added noise
e\sim N(0,0.6)
such that
Y=f_{\text{shifted}}(X)+e
. In classification, the labels of shifted samples were reversed to simulate label noise.

We experimented with shift probabilities in
\{0.0,0.05,0.1,0.15,0.2,0.25\}
, using training sets of
1000
samples. The WDRO uncertainty level was selected via cross-validation over
\{0.0,0.05,0.1,0.15,0.2,0.25\}
. An FNN with three hidden layers of sizes
32,16,
and
8
was used. The output layer consisted of a single unit for regression or a binary classifier for classification. Norm constraints were applied to all linear layers to enforce a Lipschitz constant not exceeding 10.

The methods were evaluated on both standard (unperturbed) and perturbed test sets, with the latter generated using twice the training shift probability. For classification task, an imbalanced test set was also considered (50% of class 1 labels flipped). All test sets comprised of 500 samples, and each experiment was repeated independently 20 times.

Table
